| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | Good morrow, cousin Warwick, good morrow. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | and [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] Good morrow, cousin. |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | We meet like men that had forgot to speak. |
| WARWICK | We do remember, but our argument<br>Is all too heavy to admit much talk. |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | [[Well]], peace be with him that hath made us heavy! |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | Peace be with us, lest we be heavier I |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | O good my lord, you have lost a friend indeed;<br>And I dare swear you borrow not that face<br>Of seeming sorrow—it is sure your own. |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | (to [[Lord Chief Justice]])<br>Though no man be assured what grace to find,<br>You stand in coldest expectation.<br>I am the sorrier; would ’twere otherwise. |
| [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]] | (to [[Lord Chief Justice]])<br>[[Well]], you must now speak Sir John [[Falstaff]] fair,<br>Which swims against your stream of quality. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | Sweet princes, what I did I did in [[Honour\|honour]],<br>Led by th’impartial conduct of my [[Soul\|soul]];<br>And never shall you see that I will beg<br>A ragged and forestalled remission.<br>If [[Truth\|truth]] and upright innocency fail me,<br>I’ll to the [[King]] my master, that is dead,<br>And tell him who hath sent me after him. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Prince Harry, as [[King]] |
| WARWICK | Here comes the Prince. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | Good morrow, and God save your majesty I |
| PRINCE HARRY | This new and gorgeous garment, majesty,<br>Sits not so easy on me as you think.<br>Brothers, you mix your sadness with some [[Fear\|fear]].<br>This is the English not the Turkish [[Court\|court]];<br>Not Amurath an Amurath succeeds,<br>But Harry Harry. Yet be sad, good brothers,<br>For, by my [[Faith\|faith]], it very [[Well\|well]] becomes you.<br>Sorrow so royally in you appears<br>That I will deeply put the fashion on,<br>And wear it in my heart. Why then, be sad;<br>But entertain no more of it, good brothers,<br>Than a joint burden laid upon us all.<br>For me, by heaven, I bid you be assured<br>I’ll be your father and your brother too.<br>Let me but bear your [[Love\|love]], I’ll bear your cares.<br>Yet weep that Harry’s dead, and so will I;<br>But Harry lives that shall [[convert]] those [[Tears\|tears]]<br>By number into hours of happiness. |
| PRINCE | JOHN, [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]], and [[Clarence\|CLARENCE]]<br>We [[Hope\|hope]] no other from your majesty. |
| PRINCE HARRY | You all look strangely on me, (to [[Lord Chief Justice]])<br>and you most.<br>You are, I think, assured I [[Love\|love]] you not. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | I am assured, if I be measured rightly,<br>Your majesty hath no just cause to hate me. |
| PRINCE HARRY | No? How might a prince of my great hopes forget<br>So great indignities you laid upon me?<br>What—rate, rebuke, and roughly send to [[Prison\|prison]]<br>Th’immediate heir of England? Was this easy?<br>May this be washed in Lethe and forgotten ? |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | I then did use the person of your father.<br>The image of his power lay then in me;<br>And in th’administration of his law,<br>Whiles I was busy for the commonwealth,<br>Your highness pleased to forget my place,<br>The majesty and power of law and justice,<br>The image of the [[King]] whom I presented,<br>And struck me in my very seat of judgement;<br>Whereon, as an offender to your father,<br>I gave bold way to my authority<br>And did commit you. If the deed were ill,<br>Be you contented, wearing now the garland,<br>To have a son set your decrees at naught—<br>To pluck down justice from your awe-full bench,<br>To trip the course of law, and blunt the [[Sword\|sword]]<br>That guards the peace and safety of your person,<br>Nay, more, to spurn at your most royal image,<br>And mock your workings in a second [[Body\|body]]?<br>Question your royal thoughts, make the case yours,<br>Be now the father, and propose a son;<br>Hear your own dignity so much profaned,<br>See your most dreadful laws so loosely slighted,<br>Behold yourself so by a son disdained;<br>And then imagine me taking your part,<br>And in your power soft silencing your son.<br>After this cold considerance, sentence me;<br>And, as you are a [[King\|king]], speak in your state<br>What I have done that misbecame my place,<br>My person, or my liege’s sovereignty. |
| PRINCE HARRY | You are right Justice, and you weigh this [[Well\|well]].<br>Therefore still bear the balance and the [[Sword\|sword]];<br>And I do wish your honours may increase<br>Till you do live to see a son of mine<br>Offend you and obey you as I did.<br>So shall I live to speak my father’s words:<br>‘Happy am I that have a man so bold<br>That dares do justice on my proper son,<br>And not less happy having such a son<br>That would deliver up his [[Greatness\|greatness]] so<br>Into the hands of justice.’ You did commit me,<br>For which I do commit into your hand<br>Th‘unstainèd [[Sword\|sword]] that you have used to bear,<br>With this remembrance: that you use the same<br>With the like bold, just, and impartial spirit<br>As you have done ’gainst me. There is my hand.<br>You shall be as a father to my youth;<br>My voice shall sound as you do prompt mine ear,<br>And I will stoop and humble my intents<br>To your [[Well\|well]]-practised wise directions.—<br>And princes all, believe me, I beseech you,<br>My father is gone wild into his [[Grave\|grave]],<br>For in his tomb lie my affections;<br>And with his spirits sadly I survive<br>To mock the expectation of the world,<br>To frustrate prophecies, and to raze out<br>Rotten opinion, who hath writ me down<br>After my seeming. The tide of [[Blood\|blood]] in me<br>Hath proudly flowed in [[Vanity\|vanity]] till now.<br>Now doth it turn, and ebb back to the sea,<br>Where it shall mingle with the state of floods,<br>And flow henceforth in formal majesty.<br>Now call we our high [[Court\|court]] of Parliament,<br>And let us choose such limbs of noble counsel<br>That the great [[Body\|body]] of our state may go<br>In equal rank with the best-governed nation;<br>That war, or peace, or both at once, may be<br>As things acquainted and familiar to us;<br>(To [[Lord Chief Justice]])<br>In which you, father, shall have foremost hand.<br>(To all) Our coronation done, we will accite,<br>As I before remembered, all our state;<br>And, God consigning to my good intents,<br>No prince nor peer shall have just cause to say,<br>‘God shorten Harry’s happy life one day.’ Exeunt |
| *(didascalia)* | 5.3 A table and chairs set forth. Enter Sir John [[Falstaff]], Shallow, [[Silence]], Davy With vessels for the table, Bardolph, and the Page |
| SHALLOW | (to Sir John) Nay, you shall see my orchard, where, in an arbour, we will eat a last year’s pippin of mine own grafting, with a dish of caraways, and so forth—come, cousin [[Silence]]—and then to bed. |
| SIR JOHN | Fore God, you have here a goodly dwelling and a rich. |
| SHALLOW | Barren, barren, barren; beggars all, beggars all,<br>Sir John. Marry, good air.—Spread, Davy; spread, Davy. |
| *(didascalia)* | Davy begins to spread the table |
| SHALLOW | [[Well]] said, Davy. |
| SIR JOHN | This Davy serves you for good uses; he is your serving-man and your husband. |
| SHALLOW | A good varlet, a good varlet, a very good varlet, Sir John.—By the mass, I have drunk too much sack at supper.—A good varlet. Now sit down, now sit down. (To [[Silence]]) Come, cousin. |
| [[Silence\|SILENCE]] | Ah, sirrah, quoth-a, we shall<br>(sings)<br>Do nothing but eat, and make good cheer,<br>And praise God for the merry year,<br>When flesh is cheap and females dear,<br>And lusty lads roam here and there<br>So merrily,<br>And ever among so merrily. |
| SIR JOHN | There’s a merry heart, good Master [[Silence]]! I’ll give you a health for that anon. |
| SHALLOW | Good Master Bardotph!—Some wine, Davy. |
| *(didascalia)* | DAVY to Sir john Sweet sir, sit. To Bardolph I’ll be with you anon. To Sir John Most sweet sir, sit. [[Master Page\|Master page]], good [[Master Page\|master page]], sit. |
| *(didascalia)* | All but Davy sit. Davy pours wine |
| SHALLOW | Proface! What you want in meat, we’ll have in drink; but you must bear; the heart’s all. |
| SHALLOW | Be merry, Master Bardolph and my little soldier there, be merry. |
| [[Silence\|SILENCE]] | (sings)<br>Be merry, be merry, my wife has all,<br>For women are shrews, both short and tall,<br>’Tis merry in hall when beards wags all,<br>And welcome merry shrovetide.<br>Be merry, be merry. |
| JOHN | I did not think Master [[Silence]] had been a man of this mettle. |
| [[Silence\|SILENCE]] | Who, I? I have been merry twice and once ere now. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Davy With a dish of apples |
| DAVY | There’s a dish of leather-coats for you. |
| SHALLOW | Davy! |
| *(didascalia)* | DAVY Your worship! I’ll be with you straight. To Sir John A cup of wine, sir? |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Silence\|SILENCE]] sings |
| SHALLOW | A cup of wine<br>That’s brisk and fine,<br>And drink unto thee, leman mine,<br>And a merry heart lives long-a. |
| SIR JOHN | [[Well]] said, Master [[Silence]]. |
| [[Silence\|SILENCE]] | And we shall be merry; now comes in the sweet o’th’ [[Night\|night]]. |
| SIR JOHN | Health and long life to you, Master [[Silence]]! He drinks |
| [[Silence\|SILENCE]] | Fill the cup and let it come. I’ll pledge you a mile to th’ [[Bottom\|bottom]]. |
| SHALLOW | Honest Bardolph, welcome! If thou want’st anything and wilt not call, beshrew thy heart! (To the Page) Welcome, my little tiny thief, and welcome indeed, too!-I’ll drink to Master Bardolph, and to all the cavalieros about [[London]].<br>He drinks |
| DAVY | I [[Hope\|hope]] to see [[London]] once ere I die. |
| BARDOLPH | An I might see you there, Davy! |
| SHALLOW | By the mass, you’ll crack a quart together, ha, will you not, Master Bardolph? |
| BARDOLPH | Yea, sir, in a pottle-pot. |
| SHALLOW | By God’s liggens, I thank thee. The knave will stick by thee, I can assure thee that; a will not out; ’tis true-bred. |
| BARDOLPH | And I’ll stick by him, sir. |
| SHALLOW | Why, there spoke a [[King\|king]]! Lack nothing, be merry!<br>One knocks at the door within<br>Look who’s at door there, ho! Who knocks? |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Davy |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Silence]] drinks |
| *(didascalia)* | SIR JOHN to [[Silence]] Why, now you have done me right! |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Silence\|SILENCE]] sings Do me right, |
| SHALLOW | And dub me knight—<br>Samingo.<br>Is’t not so? |
| SIR JOHN | ’Tis so. |
| [[Silence\|SILENCE]] | Is’t so?—Why then, say an old man can do somewhat. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Davy |
| DAVY | An’t please your worship, there’s one [[Pistol]] come from the [[Court\|court]] with news. |
| SIR JOHN | From the [[Court\|court]]? Let him come in. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Pistol]] |
| SIR JOHN | How now, [[Pistol]]? |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Sir John, God save you. |
| SIR JOHN | What [[Wind\|wind]] blew you hither, [[Pistol]]? |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Not the ill [[Wind\|wind]] which blows no man to good.<br>Sweet knight, thou art now one of the greatest men in<br>this realm. |
| [[Silence\|SILENCE]] | By‘r Lady, I think a be—but goodman Puff of Bar’son. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Puff?<br>Puff in thy teeth, most recreant coward base!—<br>Sir John, I am thy [[Pistol]] and thy friend,<br>And helter-skelter have I rode to thee,<br>And tidings do I bring, and lucky joys,<br>And golden times, and happy news of price. |
| SIR JOHN | I pray thee now, deliver them like a man of this world. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | A foutre for the world and worldlings base!<br>I speak of Africa and golden joys. |
| SIR JOHN | O base Assyrian knight, what is thy news?<br>Let [[King]] Cophetua know the [[Truth\|truth]] thereof. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Silence\|SILENCE]] singing |
| SIR JOHN | ‘And [[Robin Hood]], Scarlet, and John.’ |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Shall dunghill curs confront the Helicons?<br>And shall good news be baffled?<br>Then [[Pistol]] lay thy head in Furies’ lap. |
| SHALLOW | Honest gentleman, I know not your breeding. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Why then, lament therefor. |
| SHALLOW | Give me pardon, sir. If, sir, you come with news from the [[Court\|court]], I take it there’s but two ways: either to utter them, or conceal them. I am, sir, under the [[King]] in some authority. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Under which [[King\|king]], besonian? Speak, or die. |
| SHALLOW | Under [[King]] Harry. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Harry the Fourth, or Fifth? |
| SHALLOW | Harry the Fourth. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | A foutre for thine office!<br>Sir John, thy tender lambkin now is [[King\|king]].<br>Harry the Fifth’s the man. I speak the [[Truth\|truth]].<br>When [[Pistol]] lies, do this, (making the fig) and fig me,<br>Like the bragging Spaniard. |
| SIR JOHN | What, is the old [[King]] dead? |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | As nail in door. The things I speak are just. |
| SIR JOHN | Away, Bardolph, saddle my [[Horse\|horse]]! Master Robert Shallow, choose what office thou wilt in the land; ’tis thine. [[Pistol]], I will [[Double\|double]]-charge thee with dignities. |
| BARDOLPH | O joyful day!<br>I would not take a knighthood for my fortune. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | What, I do bring good news? |
| SIR JOHN | (to Davy) Carry Master [[Silence]] to bed. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Davy with [[Silence]] |
| SIR JOHN | Master Shallow—my lord Shallow—be what thou wilt, I am fortune’s steward—get on thy boots; we’ll ride all [[Night\|night]].—O sweet [[Pistol]]!—Away, Bardolph! |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Bardolph |
| SIR JOHN | Come, [[Pistol]], utter more to me, and withal devise something to do thyself good. Boot, boot, Master Shallow! I know the [[Young King\|young King]] is sick for me. Let us take any man’s horses—the laws of England are at my commandment. Blessed are they that have been my friends, and woe to my [[Lord Chief Justice]]. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Let vultures vile seize on his lungs also!<br>‘Where is the life that late I led?’ say they.<br>Why, here it is. Welcome these pleasant days. Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Beadles, dragging in [[Mistress Quickly]] and [[Doll Tearsheet]] |
| [[Mistress Quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | No, thou arrant knave! I would to God that I might die, that I might have thee hanged. Thou hast drawn my shoulder out of joint. |
| FIRST BEADLE | The constables have delivered her over to me; and she shall have whipping-cheer, I warrant her. There hath been a man or two killed about her. |
| [[Doll Tearsheet\|DOLL TEARSHEET]] | Nut-hook, nut-hook, you lie! Come on, I’ll tell thee what, thou damned tripe-visaged rascal, an the [[Child\|child]] I go with do miscarry, thou wert better thou hadst struck thy mother, thou paper-faced [[Villain\|villain]]. |
| [[Mistress Quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | O the Lord, that Sir John were come! He would make this a bloody day to somebody. But I pray God the fruit of her womb miscarry! |
| FIRST BEADLE | If it do, you shall have a dozen of cushions again; you have but eleven now. Come, I charge you both go with me, for the man is dead that you and [[Pistol]] beat amongst you. |
| [[Doll Tearsheet\|DOLL TEARSHEET]] | I’ll tell you what, you thin man in a censer, I will have you as soundly swinged for this, you bluebottle rogue, you filthy famished correctioner! If you be not swinged, I’ll forswear half-kirtles. |
| FIRST BEADLE | Come, come, you she knight-errant, come! |
| [[Mistress Quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | O God, that right should thus o’ercome might! [[Well]], of sufferance comes ease. |
| [[Doll Tearsheet\|DOLL TEARSHEET]] | Come, you rogue, come; bring me to a justice. |
| [[Mistress Quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Ay, come, you starved bloodhound. |
| [[Doll Tearsheet\|DOLL TEARSHEET]] | Goodman [[Death\|death]], goodman bones! |
| [[Mistress Quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Thou atomy, thou! |
| [[Doll Tearsheet\|DOLL TEARSHEET]] | Come, you thin thing; come, you rascal. |
| FIRST BEADLE | Very [[Well\|well]]. Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 5

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter two Grooms, strewing rushes |
| FIRST GROOM | More rushes, more rushes! |
| SECOND GROOM | The trumpets have sounded twice. |
| *(didascalia)* | FIRST GROOM ‘Twill be two o’[[Clock\|clock]] ere they come from the coronation. Exeunt |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir John [[Falstaff]], Shallow, [[Pistol]], Bardolph, and the Page |
| SIR JOHN | Stand here by me, Master Robert Shallow. I will make the [[King]] do you grace. I will leer upon him as a comes by, and do but mark the countenance that he will give me. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | God bless thy lungs, good knight. |
| SIR JOHN | Come here, [[Pistol]]; stand behind me. (To Shallow) O, if I had had [[Time\|time]] to have made new liveries, I would have bestowed the thousand pound I borrowed of you! But ’tis no matter; this poor show doth better; this doth infer the zeal I had to see him. |
| *(didascalia)* | SHALLOW It doth so. |
| SIR JOHN | It shows my earnestness of affection— |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | It doth so. |
| SIR JOHN | My devotion— |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | It doth, it doth, it doth. |
| SIR JOHN | As it were, to ride day and [[Night\|night]], and not to deliberate, not to remember, not to have patience to shift me— |
| SHALLOW | It is most certain. |
| *(didascalia)* | SIR JOHN But to stand stained with travel and sweating with [[Desire\|desire]] to see him, thinking of nothing else, putting all affairs in oblivion, as if there were nothing else to be done but to see him. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | ’Tis semper idem, for absque hoc nihil est: ’tis all in every part. |
| SHALLOW | ’Tis so indeed. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | My knight, I will inflame thy noble liver,<br>And make thee rage.<br>Thy Doll, and [[Helen]] of thy noble thoughts,<br>Is in base durance and contagious [[Prison\|prison]],<br>Haled thither<br>By most mechanical and dirty hand.<br>Rouse up [[Revenge]] from ebon den with fell Alecto’s<br>snake,<br>For Doll is in. [[Pistol]] speaks naught but [[Truth\|truth]]. |
| SIR JOHN | I will deliver her. |
| *(didascalia)* | Shouts within. Trumpets sound |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | There roared the sea, and trumpet-clangour sounds! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[King]] Harry the Fifth, [[Prince John]] of Lancaster, the Dukes of [[Clarence]] and [[Gloucester]], the [[Lord Chief Justice]], and others |
| SIR JOHN | God save thy grace, [[King]] Hal, my royal Hal! |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | The heavens thee guard and keep, most royal imp of fame! |
| SIR JOHN | God save thee, my sweet boy! |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | My [[Lord Chief Justice]], speak to that vain man. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | (to Sir John)<br>Have you your wits? Know you what ’tis you speak? |
| SIR JOHN | My [[King\|king]], my Jove, I speak to thee, my heart! |
| [[King\|KING]] HARRY | I know thee not, old man. Fall to thy prayers.<br>How ill white hairs becomes a [[Fool\|fool]] and jester!<br>I have long dreamt of such a kind of man,<br>So surfeit-swelled, so old, and so profane;<br>But being awake, I do despise my dream.<br>Make less thy [[Body\|body]] hence, and more thy grace.<br>Leave gormandizing; know the [[Grave\|grave]] doth gape<br>For thee thrice wider than for other men.<br>Reply not to me with a [[Fool\|fool]]-born jest.<br>Presume not that I am the thing I was,<br>For God doth know, so shall the world perceive,<br>That I have turned away my former [[Self\|self]];<br>So will I those that kept me company.<br>When thou dost hear I am as I have been,<br>Approach me, and thou shalt be as thou wast,<br>The tutor and the feeder of my riots.<br>Till then I banish thee, on [[Pain\|pain]] of [[Death\|death]],<br>As I have done the rest of my misleaders,<br>Not to come near our person by ten mile.<br>For competence of life I will allow you,<br>That lack of means enforce you not to evils;<br>And as we hear you do [[Reform\|reform]] yourselves,<br>We will, according to your strengths and qualities,<br>Give you advancement. (To [[Lord Chief Justice]]) Be it<br>your charge, my lord,<br>To see performed the tenor of our word. (To his train)<br>Set on! Exeunt [[King]] Harry and his train |
| SIR JOHN | Master Shallow, I owe you a thousand pound. |
| SHALLOW | Yea, marry, Sir John; which I beseech you to let me have [[Home\|home]] with me. |
| SIR JOHN | That can hardly be,, Master Shallow. Do not you grieve at this. I shall be sent for in private to him. Look you, he must seem thus to the world. [[Fear]] not your advancements. I will be the man yet that shall make you great. |
| SHALLOW | I cannot perceive how, unless you give me your doublet and stuff me out with straw. I beseech you, good Sir John, let me have five hundred of my thousand. |
| SIR JOHN | Sir, I will be as good as my word. This that you heard was but a colour. |
| SHALLOW | A colour I [[Fear\|fear]] that you will die in, Sir John. |
| SIR JOHN | [[Fear]] no colours. Go with me to dinner. Come, Lieutenant [[Pistol]]; come, Bardolph. I shall be sent for soon at [[Night\|night]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Lord Chief Justice]] and [[Prince John]], with officers |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | (to officers)<br>Go carry Sir John [[Falstaff]] to the Fleet.<br>Take all his company along with him. |
| SIR JOHN | My lord, my lord! |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | I cannot now speak. I will hear you soon.—<br>Take them away. |
| [[Pistol\|PISTOL]] | Si fortuna me tormenta, spero me contenta. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt all but [[Prince John]] and [[Lord Chief Justice]] |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | I like this fair proceeding of the [[King]]’s.<br>He hath intent his wonted followers<br>Shall all be very [[Well\|well]] provided for,<br>But all are banished till their conversations<br>Appear more wise and modest to the world. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | And so they are. |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | The [[King]] hath called his parliament, my lord. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | He hath. |
| [[Prince John\|PRINCE JOHN]] | I will lay odds that, ere this year expire,<br>We bear our civil swords and native [[Fire\|fire]]<br>As far as France. I heard a bird so sing,<br>Whose music, to my thinking, pleased the [[King]].<br>Come, will you hence? Exeunt<br><br>Epilogue |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Epilogue |
| EPILOGUE | First my [[Fear\|fear]], then my curtsy, last my speech.<br>My [[Fear\|fear]] is your displeasure; my curtsy, my duty; and my speech to beg your pardons. If you look for a good speech now, you undo me; for what I have to say is of mine own making, and what indeed I should say will, I [[Doubt\|doubt]], prove mine own marring. But to the purpose, and so to the venture. Be it known to you, as it is very [[Well\|well]], I was lately here in the end of a displeasing play, to pray your patience for it, and to promise you a better. I did mean indeed to pay you with this; which, if like an ill venture it come unluckily [[Home\|home]], I break, and you, my gentle creditors, lose. Here I promised you I would be, and here I commit my [[Body\|body]] to your mercies. Bate me some, and I will pay you some, and, as most debtors do, promise you infinitely.<br>If my tongue cannot entreat you to acquit me, will you command me to use my legs? And yet that were but light payment, to [[Dance\|dance]] out of your debt. But a good [[Conscience\|conscience]] will make any possible satisfaction, and so would I. All the gentlewomen here have forgiven me; if the gentlemen will not, then the gentlemen do not agree with the gentlewomen, which was never seen before in such an assembly.<br>One word more, I beseech you. If you be not too much cloyed with fat meat, our humble author will continue the story with Sir John in it, and make you merry with fair Catherine of France; where, for anything I know, [[Falstaff]] shall die of a sweat—unless already a be killed with your hard opinions. For Oldcastle died a martyr, and this is not the man. My tongue is weary; when my legs are too, I will bid you good [[Night\|night]], and so kneel down before you—but, indeed, to pray for the [[Queen]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | He dances, then kneels for applause. Exit |
| ADDITIONAL PASSAGES | Along with some substantial additions, Shakespeare probably made a number of short excisions when preparing the finished version of the play. The following, present in the Quarto but entirely or substantially omitted in the later Folio text, are the most significant: |
| A. AFTER | 2.2.22<br>And God knows whether those that bawl out the ruins of thy linen shall inherit his kingdom—but the midwives say the children are not in the fault, whereupon the world increases, and kindreds are mightily strengthened. |
| B. AFTER | ‘LIQUORS!’, 3.1-52<br>O, if this were seen,<br>The happiest youth, viewing his progress through,<br>What perils past, what crosses to ensue,<br>Would shut the book and sit him down and die. |
| C. AFTER | ‘FAMINE.’, 3.2.309<br>yet lecherous as a monkey; and the whores called him ‘mandrake’. A came ever in the rearward of the fashion, and sung those tunes to the overscutched hussies that he heard the carmen whistle, and sware they were his fancies or his good-nights. |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_1--scene_1" data-title="2 Henry IV — Act I, Scene 1" data-chapter="Scene 1" data-kind="scene" data-tags="topos/double,archetype/king,motif/blood,motif/death,motif/decay,motif/fire,motif/horse,motif/nature,motif/night,motif/poison,motif/time,motif/well,concept/body,concept/courage,concept/fear,concept/honour,concept/nature,concept/revenge,concept/truth,character/cain,character/henry_iv,character/hotspur,character/prince_john,character/richard,character/speed"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lord Bardolph at one door. He crosses the stage to another door |
| LORD BARDOLPH | Who keeps the gate here, ho? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Porter above |
| LORD BARDOLPH | Where is the Earl? |
| PORTER | What shall I say you are? |
| LORD BARDOLPH | Tell thou the Earl<br>That the Lord Bardolph doth attend him here. |
| PORTER | His lordship is walked forth into the orchard.<br>Please it your [[Honour\|honour]] knock but at the gate,<br>And he himself will answer. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Earl Northumberland at the other door, as sick, with a crutch and coif |
| LORD BARDOLPH | Here comes the Earl. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Porter |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | What news, Lord Bardolph? Every minute now<br>Should be the father of some stratagem.<br>The times are wild; contention, like a [[Horse\|horse]]<br>Full of high feeding, madly hath broke loose,<br>And bears down all before him. |
| LORD BARDOLPH | Noble Earl,<br>I bring you certain news from Shrewsbury. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Good, an God will. |
| LORD BARDOLPH | As good as heart can wish.<br>The [[King]] is almost wounded to the [[Death\|death]];<br>And, in the fortune of my lord your son,<br>Prince Harry slain outright; and both the Blunts<br>Killed by the hand of Douglas; young [[Prince John]]<br>And Westmorland and Stafford fled the field;<br>And Harry Monmouth’s brawn, the hulk Sir John,<br>Is prisoner to your son. O, such a day,<br>So fought, so followed, and so fairly won,<br>Came not till now to dignify the times<br>Since Caesar’s fortunes! |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | How is this derived?<br>Saw you the field? Came you from Shrewsbury? |
| LORD BARDOLPH | I spake with one, my lord, that came from thence,<br>A gentleman [[Well\|well]] bred and of good name,<br>That freely rendered me these news for true. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Travers |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Here comes my servant Travers, who I sent<br>On Tuesday last to listen after news. |
| LORD BARDOLPH | My lord, I overrode him on the way,<br>And he is furnished with no certainties<br>More than he haply may retail from me. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Now, Travers, what good tidings comes with you? |
| TRAVERS | My lord, Lord Bardolph turned me back<br>With joyful tidings, and being better horsed<br>Outrode me. After him came spurring hard<br>A gentleman almost forspent with [[Speed\|speed]],<br>That stopped by me to breathe his bloodied [[Horse\|horse]].<br>He asked the way to Chester, and of him<br>I did demand what news from Shrewsbury.<br>He told me that rebellion had ill luck,<br>And that young Harry Percy’s spur was cold.<br>With that he gave his able [[Horse\|horse]] the head,<br>And, bending forward, struck his armed heels<br>Against the panting sides of his poor jade<br>Up to the rowel-head; and starting so,<br>He seemed in running to devour the way,<br>Staying no longer question. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Ha? Again:<br>Said he young Harry Percy’s spur was cold?<br>Of [[Hotspur]], ‘Coldspur’ ? that rebellion<br>Had met ill luck? |
| LORD BARDOLPH | My lord, I’ll tell you what:<br>If my young lord your son have not the day,<br>Upon mine [[Honour\|honour]], for a silken point<br>I’ll give my barony. Never talk of it. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Why should the gentleman that rode by Travers<br>Give then such instances of loss? |
| LORD BARDOLPH | Who, he?<br>He was some hilding fellow that had stol’n<br>The [[Horse\|horse]] he rode on, and, upon my life,<br>Spoke at a venture. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Morton |
| LORD BARDOLPH | Look, here comes more news. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Yea, this man’s brow, like to a title leaf,<br>Foretells the [[Nature\|nature]] of a tragic volume.<br>So looks the strand whereon the imperious flood<br>Hath left a witnessed usurpation.<br>Say, Morton, didst thou come from Shrewsbury? |
| MORTON | I ran from Shrewsbury, my noble lord,<br>Where hateful [[Death\|death]] put on his ugliest mask<br>To fright our party. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | How doth my son and brother?<br>Thou tremblest, and the whiteness in thy cheek<br>Is apter than thy tongue to tell thy errand.<br>Even such a man, so faint, so spiritless,<br>So dull, so dead in look, so woebegone,<br>Drew Priam’s curtain in the dead of [[Night\|night]],<br>And would have told him half his Troy was burnt;<br>But Priam found the [[Fire\|fire]] ere he his tongue,<br>And I my Percy’s [[Death\|death]] ere thou report‘st it.<br>This thou wouldst say: ‘Your son did thus and thus,<br>Your brother thus; so fought the noble Douglas’,<br>Stopping my greedy ear with their bold deeds;<br>But in the end, to stop my ear indeed,<br>Thou hast a sigh to blow away this praise,<br>Ending with ‘Brother, son, and all are dead.’ |
| MORTON | Douglas is living, and your brother yet;<br>But for my lord your son— |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Why, he is dead.<br>See what a ready tongue suspicion hath!<br>He that but fears the thing he would not know<br>Hath by instinct knowledge from others’ eyes<br>That what he feared is chanced. Yet speak, Morton.<br>Tell thou an earl his divination lies,<br>And I will take it as a sweet disgrace,<br>And make thee rich for doing me such wrong. |
| MORTON | You are too great to be by me gainsaid,<br>Your spirit is too true, your fears too certain. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Yet for all this, say not that Percy’s dead.<br>I see a strange confession in thine eye—<br>Thou shak‘st thy head, and hold’st it [[Fear\|fear]] or sin<br>To speak a [[Truth\|truth]]. If he be slain, say so.<br>The tongue offends not that reports his [[Death\|death]];<br>And he doth sin that doth belie the dead,<br>Not he which says the dead is not alive.<br>Yet the first bringer of unwelcome news<br>Hath but a losing office, and his tongue |
| *(didascalia)* | Sounds ever after as a sullen bell |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Remembered knolling a departing friend. |
| LORD BARDOLPH | I cannot think, my lord, your son is dead. |
| MORTON | (to Northumberland)<br>I am sorry I should force you to believe<br>That which I would to God I had not seen;<br>But these mine eyes saw him in bloody state,<br>Rend‘ring faint quittance, wearied and out-breathed,<br>To Harry Monmouth, whose swift wrath beat down<br>The never-daunted Percy to the earth,<br>From whence with life he never more sprung up.<br>In few, his [[Death\|death]], whose spirit lent a [[Fire\|fire]]<br>Even to the dullest peasant in his camp,<br>Being bruited once, took [[Fire\|fire]] and heat away<br>From the best-tempered [[Courage\|courage]] in his troops;<br>For from his metal was his party steeled,<br>Which once in him abated, all the rest<br>Turned on themselves, like dull and heavy lead;<br>And, as the thing that’s heavy in itself<br>Upon enforcement flies with greatest [[Speed\|speed]],<br>So did our men, heavy in [[Hotspur]]’s loss,<br>Lend to this weight such lightness with their [[Fear\|fear]]<br>That arrows fled not swifter toward their aim<br>Than did our soldiers, aiming at their safety,<br>Fly from the field. Then was that noble Worcester<br>Too soon ta’en prisoner; and that furious Scot<br>The bloody Douglas, whose [[Well\|well]]-labouring [[Sword\|sword]]<br>Had three times slain th’appearance of the [[King]],<br>Gan vail his stomach, and did grace the shame<br>Of those that turned their backs, and in his flight,<br>Stumbling in [[Fear\|fear]], was took. The sum of all<br>Is that the [[King]] hath won, and hath sent out<br>A speedy power to encounter you, my lord,<br>Under the conduct of young Lancaster<br>And Westmorland. This is the news at full. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | For this I shall have [[Time\|time]] enough to mourn.<br>In [[Poison\|poison]] there is physic; and these news,<br>Having been [[Well\|well]], that would have made me sick,<br>Being sick, have in some measure made me [[Well\|well]];<br>And, as the wretch whose fever-weakened joints,<br>Like strengthless hinges, buckle under life,<br>Impatient of his fit, breaks like a [[Fire\|fire]]<br>Out of his keeper’s arms, even so my limbs,<br>Weakened with grief, being now enraged with grief,<br>Are thrice themselves. |
| *(didascalia)* | He casts away his crutch |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Hence therefore, thou nice crutch!<br>A scaly gauntlet now with joints of steel<br>Must glove this hand. |
| *(didascalia)* | He snatches off his coif |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | And hence, thou sickly coif!<br>Thou art a guard too wanton for the head<br>Which princes fleshed with conquest aim to hit.<br>Now bind my brows with iron, and approach<br>The ragged‘st hour that [[Time\|time]] and spite dare bring<br>To frown upon th’enraged Northumberland!<br>Let heaven kiss earth ! Now let not [[Nature\|nature]]’s hand<br>Keep the wild flood confined! Let order die!<br>And let this world no longer be a stage<br>To feed contention in a ling’ring act;<br>But let one spirit of the first-born [[Cain]]<br>Reign in all bosoms, that each heart being set<br>On bloody courses, the rude scene may end,<br>And darkness be the burier of the dead! |
| LORD BARDOLPH | Sweet Earl, [[divorce]] not wisdom from your [[Honour\|honour]]. |
| MORTON | The lives of all your loving complices<br>Lean on your health, the which, if you give o‘er<br>To stormy passion, must perforce [[Decay\|decay]].<br>You cast th’event of war, my noble lord,<br>And summed the account of chance, before you said<br>‘Let us make head’. It was your presurmise<br>That in the dole of blows your son might drop.<br>You knew he walked o‘er perils on an edge,<br>More likely to fall in than to get o’er.<br>You were advised his flesh was capable<br>Of wounds and scars, and that his forward spirit<br>Would lift him where most trade of danger ranged.<br>Yet did you say, ‘Go forth’; and none of this,<br>Though strongly apprehended, could restrain<br>The stiff-borne action. What hath then befall’n?<br>Or what doth this bold enterprise bring forth,<br>More than that being which was like to be? |
| LORD BARDOLPH | We all that are engaged to this loss<br>Knew that we ventured on such dangerous seas<br>That if we wrought out life was ten to one;<br>And yet we ventured for the gain proposed,<br>Choked the respect of likely peril feared;<br>And since we are o’erset, venture again.<br>Come, we will all put forth [[Body\|body]] and goods. |
| MORTON | ‘Tis more than [[Time\|time]]; and, my most noble lord,<br>I hear for certain, and dare speak the [[Truth\|truth]],<br>The gentle Archbishop of York is up<br>With [[Well\|well]]-appointed powers. He is a man<br>Who with a [[Double\|double]] surety binds his followers.<br>My lord, your son had only but the corpse,<br>But shadows and the shows of men, to fight;<br>For that same word ‘rebellion’ did divide<br>The action of their bodies from their souls,<br>And they did fight with queasiness, constrained,<br>As men drink potions, that their weapons only<br>Seemed on our side; but, for their spirits and souls,<br>This word ‘rebellion’, it had froze them up,<br>As fish are in a pond. But now the Bishop<br>Turns insurrection to religion.<br>Supposed sincere and holy in his thoughts,<br>He’s followed both with [[Body\|body]] and with mind,<br>And doth enlarge his rising with the [[Blood\|blood]]<br>Of fair [[King]] [[Richard]], scraped from Pomfret stones;<br>Derives from heaven his quarrel and his cause;<br>Tells them he doth bestride a bleeding land<br>Gasping for life under great Bolingbroke;<br>And more and less do flock to follow him. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | I knew of this before, but, to speak [[Truth\|truth]],<br>This present grief had wiped it from my mind.<br>Go in with me, and counsel every man<br>The aptest way for safety and [[Revenge\|revenge]].<br>Get posts and letters, and make friends with [[Speed\|speed]].<br>Never so few, and never yet more need. Exeunt |


<span class="qlang-split" data-lang="it"></span>

| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Entra Lord Bardolph da una porta. Attraversa la scena verso un'altra porta |
| LORD BARDOLPH | Chi custodisce qui la porta, olà? |
| *(didascalia)* | Entra il Portiere in alto |
| LORD BARDOLPH | Dov'è il Conte? |
| PORTIERE | Chi devo dire che siete? |
| LORD BARDOLPH | Di' tu al Conte<br>che Lord Bardolph qui l'attende. |
| PORTIERE | Sua signoria è uscita a passeggio nel frutteto.<br>Piaccia al vostro [[Honour|onore]] bussare soltanto al cancello,<br>ed egli stesso risponderà. |
| *(didascalia)* | Entra il Conte di Northumberland dall'altra porta, come infermo, con una gruccia e una cuffia |
| LORD BARDOLPH | Ecco che viene il Conte. |
| *(didascalia)* | Esce il Portiere |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Che nuove, Lord Bardolph? Ogni minuto ormai<br>dovrebbe essere il padre di qualche stratagemma.<br>I tempi sono selvaggi; la discordia, come un [[Horse|cavallo]]<br>rimpinzato di ricca biada, follemente s'è sciolta,<br>e abbatte tutto quanto le sta innanzi. |
| LORD BARDOLPH | Nobile Conte,<br>vi reco notizie certe da Shrewsbury. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Buone, se Dio vuole. |
| LORD BARDOLPH | Buone quanto cuore possa bramare.<br>Il [[King|Re]] è quasi ferito a [[Death|morte]];<br>e, per la fortuna del signor mio vostro figlio,<br>il principe Harry ucciso di netto; ed entrambi i Blunt<br>uccisi dalla mano di Douglas; il giovane [[Prince John|principe Giovanni]]<br>e Westmorland e Stafford fuggiti dal campo;<br>e il lardo di Harry Monmouth, quel bastimento di Sir John,<br>è prigioniero di vostro figlio. Oh, un giorno tale,<br>così combattuto, così incalzato, e così bellamente vinto,<br>non venne fino ad ora a nobilitare i tempi<br>dopo le fortune di Cesare! |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Donde viene tutto questo?<br>Vedeste il campo? Veniste da Shrewsbury? |
| LORD BARDOLPH | Parlai con uno, mio signore, che di là veniva,<br>un gentiluomo [[Well|bene]] nato e di buon nome,<br>che liberamente mi diede queste nuove per vere. |
| *(didascalia)* | Entra Travers |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Ecco che viene il mio servo Travers, che mandai<br>martedì scorso a spiare notizie. |
| LORD BARDOLPH | Mio signore, io l'ho sorpassato per via,<br>ed egli non è fornito di certezza alcuna<br>più di quanto per caso possa ridire da me. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Or dunque, Travers, quali buone novelle rechi con te? |
| TRAVERS | Mio signore, Lord Bardolph mi fece tornare indietro<br>con liete novelle, e, meglio incavalcato,<br>mi sorpassò. Dietro a lui venne spronando forte<br>un gentiluomo quasi sfinito dalla [[Speed|velocità]],<br>che si fermò presso di me per far respirare il suo insanguinato [[Horse|cavallo]].<br>Chiese la strada per Chester, e a lui<br>io domandai che nuove da Shrewsbury.<br>Mi disse che la ribellione aveva avuto mala sorte,<br>e che lo sprone del giovane Harry Percy era freddo.<br>Con ciò diede briglia sciolta al suo gagliardo [[Horse|cavallo]],<br>e, chinandosi innanzi, conficcò i calcagni armati<br>nei fianchi ansanti della sua povera rozza<br>fino al perno dello sprone; e così avviatosi,<br>parve nel correre divorare la via,<br>senza attendere altra domanda. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Ah? Ancora:<br>disse che lo sprone del giovane Harry Percy era freddo?<br>Di [[Hotspur]], ‘Coldspur’ ? che la ribellione<br>avesse incontrato mala sorte? |
| LORD BARDOLPH | Mio signore, vi dirò io:<br>se il giovane signore vostro figlio non ha vinto la giornata,<br>sul mio [[Honour|onore]], per un laccio di seta<br>do la mia baronia. Non se ne parli più. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Perché dunque il gentiluomo che passò accanto a Travers<br>dava tali particolari della disfatta? |
| LORD BARDOLPH | Chi, lui?<br>Era qualche pezzente che aveva rubato<br>il [[Horse|cavallo]] che cavalcava, e, sulla mia vita,<br>parlava a casaccio. |
| *(didascalia)* | Entra Morton |
| LORD BARDOLPH | Guardate, ecco che vengono altre notizie. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Sì, la fronte di quest'uomo, come un frontespizio,<br>preannuncia la [[Nature|natura]] d'un tragico volume.<br>Tale appare la riva su cui l'imperiosa piena<br>ha lasciato testimonianza della sua usurpazione.<br>Di', Morton, venisti tu da Shrewsbury? |
| MORTON | Fuggii da Shrewsbury, mio nobile signore,<br>dove l'odiosa [[Death|morte]] indossò la sua maschera più orrenda<br>per atterrire i nostri. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Come stanno mio figlio e mio fratello?<br>Tu tremi, e il pallore della tua guancia<br>è più atto della tua lingua a dire il tuo messaggio.<br>Proprio un uomo tale, così spossato, così senza spirito,<br>così spento, così morto nello sguardo, così desolato,<br>scostò la cortina di Priamo nel cuore della [[Night|notte]],<br>e stava per dirgli che mezza Troia era arsa;<br>ma Priamo trovò il [[Fire|fuoco]] prima ch'ei la lingua,<br>e io la [[Death|morte]] del mio Percy prima che tu la riferisca.<br>Questo vorresti dire: ‘Vostro figlio fece così e così,<br>vostro fratello così; così combatté il nobile Douglas’,<br>colmando il mio avido orecchio delle loro audaci gesta;<br>ma alla fine, per turarmi l'orecchio davvero,<br>hai un sospiro che spazzerà via questa lode,<br>chiudendo con ‘Fratello, figlio, e tutti sono morti.’ |
| MORTON | Douglas è vivo, e vostro fratello ancora;<br>ma quanto al signor mio vostro figlio— |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Ebbene, è morto.<br>Vedi che lingua pronta ha il sospetto!<br>Chi solo teme la cosa che non vorrebbe sapere<br>ha per istinto, dagli occhi altrui, la certezza<br>che ciò che temeva è accaduto. Pure parla, Morton.<br>Di' a un conte che la sua divinazione mente,<br>e io lo prenderò per un dolce affronto,<br>e ti farò ricco d'avermi fatto tal torto. |
| MORTON | Siete troppo grande perché io vi contraddica,<br>il vostro spirito è troppo verace, i vostri timori troppo certi. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Eppure, con tutto ciò, non dire che Percy è morto.<br>Vedo una strana confessione nel tuo occhio—<br>scuoti il capo, e tieni per [[Fear|timore]] o peccato<br>il dire una [[Truth|verità]]. S'egli è ucciso, dillo.<br>Non offende la lingua che riferisce la sua [[Death|morte]];<br>e pecca colui che calunnia il morto,<br>non chi dice che il morto non è vivo.<br>Pure il primo latore d'una nuova sgradita<br>ha soltanto un ufficio perdente, e la sua lingua |
| *(didascalia)* | suona per sempre poi come una cupa campana |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | ricordata mentre rintocca per un amico che si parte. |
| LORD BARDOLPH | Non posso credere, mio signore, che vostro figlio sia morto. |
| MORTON | (a Northumberland)<br>Mi duole di dovervi costringere a credere<br>ciò che, piacesse a Dio, non avessi veduto;<br>ma questi miei occhi lo videro in stato sanguinoso,<br>rendendo fiacca risposta, stanco e senza fiato,<br>a Harry Monmouth, la cui rapida ira abbatté<br>al suolo il mai domato Percy,<br>donde vivo non si rialzò mai più.<br>In breve, la sua [[Death|morte]], il cui spirito prestava un [[Fire|fuoco]]<br>persino al più tardo villano del suo campo,<br>una volta divulgata, tolse [[Fire|fuoco]] e calore<br>al meglio temprato [[Courage|coraggio]] delle sue schiere;<br>ché dal suo metallo era acciaiato il suo partito,<br>e quello, una volta in lui scemato, tutto il resto<br>si volse contro se stesso, come piombo greve e opaco;<br>e, come la cosa che è greve di per sé<br>lanciata a forza vola con la massima [[Speed|velocità]],<br>così i nostri uomini, gravi per la perdita di [[Hotspur]],<br>diedero a tal peso tale leggerezza col loro [[Fear|timore]]<br>che le frecce non volarono più rapide al bersaglio<br>di quanto i nostri soldati, mirando alla salvezza,<br>fuggirono dal campo. Allora quel nobile Worcester<br>fu troppo presto fatto prigioniero; e quel furioso scozzese,<br>il sanguinario Douglas, la cui [[Sword|spada]] [[Well|ben]] menata<br>aveva tre volte ucciso il sembiante del [[King|Re]],<br>venne meno d'animo, e onorò la vergogna<br>di quei che volsero le spalle, e nella sua fuga,<br>inciampando nel [[Fear|timore]], fu preso. La somma di tutto<br>è che il [[King|Re]] ha vinto, e ha inviato<br>un rapido esercito ad affrontarvi, mio signore,<br>sotto la condotta del giovane Lancaster<br>e di Westmorland. Queste sono le nuove per intero. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Per questo avrò [[Time|tempo]] abbastanza di piangere.<br>Nel [[Poison|veleno]] c'è medicina; e queste nuove,<br>che, se fossi stato [[Well|sano]], m'avrebbero fatto ammalare,<br>essendo io malato, m'hanno in qualche misura fatto [[Well|sano]];<br>e come lo sciagurato le cui giunture indebolite dalla febbre,<br>simili a cardini senza forza, si piegano sotto la vita,<br>impaziente del suo accesso, prorompe come un [[Fire|fuoco]]<br>dalle braccia del suo custode, così le mie membra,<br>fiaccate dal dolore, ora infuriate dal dolore,<br>sono tre volte se stesse. |
| *(didascalia)* | Getta via la gruccia |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Via dunque, tu molle gruccia!<br>Un guanto squamoso ora con giunture d'acciaio<br>deve inguantare questa mano. |
| *(didascalia)* | Si strappa la cuffia |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | E via, tu cuffia da infermo!<br>Sei una difesa troppo delicata per il capo<br>che principi inaspriti dalla conquista mirano a colpire.<br>Or cingetemi le tempie di ferro, e s'avvicini<br>l'ora più orrida che il [[Time|tempo]] e il livore osino portare<br>a corrugarsi sull'infuriato Northumberland!<br>Baci il cielo la terra! Or non tenga la mano della [[Nature|natura]]<br>rinchiusa la piena selvaggia! Muoia l'ordine!<br>E non sia più questo mondo un palcoscenico<br>che nutra la discordia in un atto che si trascina;<br>ma un solo spirito, quello del primogenito [[Cain|Caino]],<br>regni in tutti i petti, sì che ogni cuore, volto<br>a sanguinose imprese, ponga fine alla rozza scena,<br>e le tenebre siano il seppellitore dei morti! |
| LORD BARDOLPH | Dolce Conte, non [[divorce|divorziate]] la saggezza dal vostro [[Honour|onore]]. |
| MORTON | Le vite di tutti i vostri amorevoli complici<br>dipendono dalla vostra salute, la quale, se v'abbandonate<br>a tempestosa passione, dovrà per forza [[Decay|decadere]].<br>Voi calcolaste l'esito della guerra, mio nobile signore,<br>e faceste il conto del caso, prima di dire<br>‘Muoviamo in armi’. Fu vostra previsione<br>che nella distribuzione dei colpi vostro figlio potesse cadere.<br>Sapevate ch'egli camminava sui pericoli come su una lama,<br>più facile a cadervi dentro che a passare oltre.<br>Eravate avvertito che la sua carne era capace<br>di ferite e cicatrici, e che il suo spirito impetuoso<br>l'avrebbe portato dove più s'affollava il pericolo.<br>Pure diceste: ‘Va’; e nulla di tutto questo,<br>benché fortemente presentito, poté trattenere<br>l'ostinata impresa. Che cosa dunque è accaduto?<br>O che cosa reca questa ardita impresa,<br>più di quell'essere che era probabile fosse? |
| LORD BARDOLPH | Noi tutti che siamo impegnati in questa perdita<br>sapevamo che ci avventuravamo su mari sì pericolosi<br>che se ne uscivamo vivi era dieci contro uno;<br>eppure ci avventurammo per il guadagno proposto,<br>soffocando il riguardo del probabile pericolo temuto;<br>e poiché siamo rovesciati, avventuriamoci di nuovo.<br>Su, metteremo tutti in gioco [[Body|corpo]] e beni. |
| MORTON | È più che [[Time|tempo]]; e, mio nobilissimo signore,<br>odo per certo, e oso dire il [[Truth|vero]],<br>che il gentile Arcivescovo di York è insorto<br>con forze [[Well|bene]] agguerrite. È un uomo<br>che con una [[Double|doppia]] sicurtà lega i suoi seguaci.<br>Mio signore, vostro figlio aveva soltanto i corpi,<br>solo ombre e parvenze d'uomini, per combattere;<br>ché quella stessa parola ‘ribellione’ divideva<br>l'azione dei loro corpi dalle loro anime,<br>ed essi combattevano con ripugnanza, forzati,<br>come uomini che bevono pozioni, sì che le loro armi soltanto<br>parevano dalla nostra parte; ma quanto agli spiriti e alle anime,<br>quella parola ‘ribellione’ li aveva agghiacciati,<br>come pesci in uno stagno. Ma ora il Vescovo<br>muta l'insurrezione in religione.<br>Creduto sincero e santo nei suoi pensieri,<br>è seguito e col [[Body|corpo]] e con la mente,<br>e ingrossa la sua rivolta col [[Blood|sangue]]<br>del bel [[King|re]] [[Richard|Riccardo]], raschiato dalle pietre di Pomfret;<br>fa derivare dal cielo la sua querela e la sua causa;<br>dice loro ch'egli sta a cavalcioni d'una terra sanguinante<br>che ansima per la vita sotto il grande Bolingbroke;<br>e grandi e piccoli accorrono a seguirlo. |
| NORTHUMBERLAND | Sapevo di ciò già prima, ma, a dire il [[Truth|vero]],<br>questo presente dolore me l'aveva cancellato dalla mente.<br>Venite dentro con me, e ciascuno consigli<br>la via più adatta alla salvezza e alla [[Revenge|vendetta]].<br>Mandate corrieri e lettere, e fatevi amici con [[Speed|prestezza]].<br>Mai così pochi, e mai finora maggior bisogno. Escono |



<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_1--scene_2" data-title="2 Henry IV — Act I, Scene 2" data-chapter="Scene 2" data-kind="scene" data-tags="topos/double,archetype/king,motif/blood,motif/clock,motif/death,motif/hill,motif/home,motif/horse,motif/jewel,motif/lion,motif/night,motif/sleep,motif/time,motif/water,motif/well,motif/wind,concept/hope,concept/imprisonment,concept/poverty,concept/pride,concept/truth,character/earl_of_northumberland,character/falstaff,character/henry_iv,character/lord_chief_justice"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir John [[Falstaff]], followed by his Page bearing his [[Sword\|sword]] and buckler |
| SIR JOHN | Sirrah, you giant, what says the doctor to my [[Water\|water]]? |
| PAGE | He said, sir, the [[Water\|water]] itself was a good healthy [[Water\|water]], but, for the party that owed it, he might have more diseases than he knew for. |
| SIR JOHN | Men of all sorts take a [[Pride\|pride]] to gird at me. The brain of this foolish-compounded clay, man, is not able to invent anything that tends to laughter more than I invent, or is invented on me. I am not only witty in myself, but the cause that wit is in other men. I do here walk before thee like a sow that hath o‘erwhelmed all her litter but one. If the Prince put thee into my service for any other reason than to set me off, why then, I have no judgement. Thou whoreson mandrake, thou art fitter to be worn in my cap than to wait at my heels. I was never manned with an agate till now; but I will set you neither in gold nor silver, but in vile apparel, and send you back again to your master for a [[Jewel\|jewel]]—the juvenal the Prince your master, whose chin is not yet fledge. I will sooner have a beard grow in the palm of my hand than he shall get one off his cheek; and yet he will not stick to say his face is a face-royal. God may finish it when he will; ’tis not a hair amiss yet. He may keep it still at a face-royal, for a barber shall never earn sixpence out of it. And yet he’ll be crowing as if he had writ man ever since his father was a bachelor. He may keep his own grace, but he’s almost out of mine, I can assure him. What said Master Dumbleton about the satin for my short cloak and slops? |
| PAGE | He said, sir, you should procure him better assurance than Bardolph. He would not take his bond and yours; he liked not the security. |
| SIR JOHN | Let him be damned like the glutton! Pray God his tongue be hotter! A whoreson Achitophel, a rascally yea-forsooth knave, to bear a gentleman in hand and then stand upon security! The whoreson smooth-pates do now wear nothing but high shoes and bunches of keys at their girdles; and if a man is through with them in honest taking-up, then they must stand upon security. I had as lief they would put ratsbane in my mouth as offer to stop it with security. I looked a should have sent me two-and-twenty yards of satin, as I am a true knight, and he sends me ‘security’! [[Well]], he may [[Sleep\|sleep]] in security, for he hath the horn of abundance, and the lightness of his wife shines through it; and yet cannot he see, though he have his own lanthorn to light him. Where’s Bardolph? |
| PAGE | He’s gone in Smithfield to buy your worship a [[Horse\|horse]]. |
| SIR JOHN | I bought him in Paul’s, and he’ll buy me a [[Horse\|horse]] in Smithfield. An I could get me but a wife in the stews, I were manned, horsed, and wived. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Lord Chief Justice]] and his Servant |
| PAGE | Sir, here comes the nobleman that committed the<br>Prince for striking him about Bardolph. |
| *(didascalia)* | SIR JOHN moving away Wait close; I will not see him. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | (to his Servant) What’s he that goes there ? |
| SERVANT | [[Falstaff]], an’t please your lordship. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | He that was in question for the robbery? |
| SERVANT | He, my lord; but he hath since done good service at Shrewsbury, and, as I hear, is now going with some charge to the Lord John of Lancaster. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | What, to York? Call him back again. |
| SERVANT | Sir John [[Falstaff]]! |
| SIR JOHN | Boy, tell him I am deaf. |
| PAGE | (to the Servant) You must speak louder; my master is deaf. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | I am sure he is to the hearing of anything good. (To the Servant) Go pluck him by the elbow; I must speak with him. |
| SERVANT | Sir John! |
| SIR JOHN | What, a young knave and begging! Is there not wars? Is there not employment? Doth not the [[King]] lack subjects? Do not the rebels want soldiers? Though it be a shame to be on any side but one, it is worse shame to beg than to be on the worst side, were it worse than the name of rebellion can tell how to make it. |
| SERVANT | You mistake me, sir. |
| SIR JOHN | Why, sir, did I say you were an honest man? Setting my knighthood and my soldiership aside, I had lied in my throat if I had said so. |
| SERVANT | I pray you, sir, then set your knighthood and your soldiership aside, and give me leave to tell you you lie in your throat if you say I am any other than an honest man. 88 |
| SIR JOHN | I give thee leave to tell me so? I lay aside that which grows to me? If thou gettest any leave of me, hang me. If thou takest leave, thou wert better be hanged. You hunt counter. Hence, avaunt! |
| SERVANT | Sir, my lord would speak with you. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | Sir John [[Falstaff]], a word with you. |
| SIR JOHN | My good lord! God give your lordship good [[Time\|time]] of day. I am glad to see your lordship abroad. I heard say your lordship was sick. I [[Hope\|hope]] your lordship goes abroad by advice. Your lordship, though not clean past your youth, have yet some smack of age in you, some relish of the saltness of [[Time\|time]] in you; and I most humbly beseech your lordship to have a reverent care of your health. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | Sir John, I sent for you before your expedition to Shrewsbury. |
| SIR JOHN | An’t please your lordship, I hear his majesty is returned with some discomfort from Wales. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | I talk not of his majesty. You would not come when I sent for you. |
| SIR JOHN | And I hear, moreover, his highness is fallen into this same whoreson apoplexy. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | [[Well]], God mend him! I pray you, let me speak with you. |
| SIR JOHN | This apoplexy is, as I take it, a kind of lethargy, an’t please your lordship, a kind of sleeping in the [[Blood\|blood]], a whoreson tingling. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | What tell you me of it? Be it as it is. |
| SIR JOHN | It hath it original from much grief, from study, and perturbation of the brain. I have read the cause of his effects in Galen. It is a kind of deafness. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | I think you are fallen into the disease, for you hear not what I say to you. |
| SIR JOHN | Very [[Well\|well]], my lord, very [[Well\|well]]. Rather, an’t please you, it is the disease of not listening, the malady of not marking, that I am troubled withal. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | To punish you by the heels would amend the attention of your ears, and I care not if I do become your physician. |
| SIR JOHN | I am as poor as Job, my lord, but not so patient.<br>Your lordship may minister the potion of [[Imprisonment\|imprisonment]] to me in respect of [[Poverty\|poverty]]; but how I should be your patient to follow your prescriptions, the wise may make some dram of a scruple, or indeed a scruple itself. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | I sent for you, when there were matters against you for your life, to come speak with me. |
| SIR JOHN | As I was then advised by my learned counsel in the laws of this land-service, I did not come. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | [[Well]], the [[Truth\|truth]] is, Sir John, you live in great infamy. |
| SIR JOHN | He that buckles himself in my belt cannot live in less. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | Your means are very slender, and your waste is great. |
| SIR JOHN | I would it were otherwise; I would my means were greater and my waist slenderer. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | You have misled the youthful Prince. |
| SIR JOHN | The young Prince hath misled me. I am the fellow with the great belly, and he my dog. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | [[Well]], I am loath to gall a new-healed wound. Your day’s service at Shrewsbury hath a little gilded over your [[Night\|night]]’s exploit on Gads [[Hill]]. You may thank th‘unquiet [[Time\|time]] for your quiet o’erposting that action. |
| SIR JOHN | My lord— |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | But since all is [[Well\|well]], keep it so. Wake not a sleeping wolf. |
| SIR JOHN | To wake a wolf is as bad as smell a fox. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | What! You are as a candle, the better part burnt out. |
| SIR JOHN | A wassail candle, my lord, all tallow—if I did say of wax, my growth would approve the [[Truth\|truth]]. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | There is not a white hair in your face but should have his effect of gravity. |
| SIR JOHN | His effect of gravy, gravy, gravy. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | You follow the young Prince up and down like his ill angel. |
| SIR JOHN | Not so, my lord; your ill angel is light, but I [[Hope\|hope]] he that looks upon me will take me without weighing. And yet in some respects, I grant, I cannot go. I cannot tell, virtue is of so little regard in these costermongers’ times that true valour is turned bearherd; pregnancy is made a tapster, and his quick wit wasted in giving reckonings; all the other gifts appertinent to man, as the malice of this age shapes them, are not worth a gooseberry. You that are old consider not the capacities of us that are young. You do measure the heat of our livers with the bitterness of your galls. And we that are in the vanguard of our youth, I must confess, are wags too. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | Do you set down your name in the scroll of youth, that are written down old with all the characters of age? Have you not a moist eye, a dry hand, a yellow cheek, a white beard, a decreasing leg, an increasing belly? Is not your voice broken, your [[Wind\|wind]] short, your chin [[Double\|double]], your wit single, and every part about you blasted with antiquity? And will you yet call yourself young? Fie, fie, fie, Sir John! |
| *(didascalia)* | SIR JOHN My lord, I was born about three of the [[Clock\|clock]] in the afternoon with a white head, and something a round belly. For my voice, I have lost it with hallowing and singing of anthems. To approve my youth further, I will not. The [[Truth\|truth]] is, I am only old in judgement and understanding; and he that will caper with me for a thousand marks, let him lend me the money, and have at him! For the box of th’ear that the Prince gave you, he gave it like a rude prince, and you took it like a sensible lord. I have checked him for it, and the young [[Lion\|lion]] repents—aside marry, not in ashes and sackcloth, but in new silk and old sack. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | [[Well]], God send the Prince a better companion! |
| SIR JOHN | God send the companion a better prince! I cannot rid my hands of him. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | [[Well]], the [[King]] hath severed you and Prince Harry. I hear you are going with Lord John of Lancaster against the Archbishop and the [[Earl of Northumberland]]. |
| SIR JOHN | Yea, I thank your pretty sweet wit for it. But look you pray, all you that kiss my lady Peace at [[Home\|home]], that our armies join not in a hot day; for, by the Lord, I take but two shirts out with me, and I mean not to sweat extraordinarily. If it be a hot day and I brandish anything but my bottle, would I might never spit white again. There is not a dangerous action can peep out his head but I am thrust upon it. [[Well]], I cannot last ever. But it was alway yet the trick of our English nation, if they have a good thing, to make it too common. If ye will needs say I am an old man, you should give me rest. I would to God my name were not so terrible to the enemy as it is. I were better to be eaten to [[Death\|death]] with a rust than to be scoured to nothing with perpetual motion. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | [[Well]], be honest, be honest, and God bless your expedition. |
| SIR JOHN | Will your lordship lend me a thousand pound to furnish me forth? |
| [[Lord Chief Justice\|LORD CHIEF JUSTICE]] | Not a penny, not a penny. You are too impatient to bear crosses. Fare you [[Well\|well]]. Commend me to my cousin Westmorland. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[Lord Chief Justice]] and his Servant |
| SIR JOHN | If I do, fillip me with a three-man beetle. A man can no more separate age and covetousness than a can part young limbs and lechery; but the gout galls the one and the pox pinches the other, and so both the degrees prevent my curses. Boy! |
| PAGE | Sir. |
| SIR JOHN | What money is in my purse? |
| PAGE | Seven groats and two pence. |
| *(didascalia)* | SIR JOHN I can get no remedy against this consumption of the purse. Borrowing only lingers and lingers it out, but the disease is incurable. (Giving letters) Go bear this letter to my lord of Lancaster; this to the Prince; this to the Earl of Westmorland; and this to old Mistress Ursula, whom I have weekly sworn to marry since I perceived the first white hair of my chin. About it. You know where to find me. Exit Pagel A pox of this gout!—or a gout of this pox!—for the one or the other plays the rogue with my great toe. ’Tis no matter if I do halt; I have the wars for my colour, and my pension shall seem the more reasonable. A good wit will make use of anything. I will turn diseases to commodity. Exit |


<span class="qlang-split" data-lang="it"></span>

| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Entra Sir John [[Falstaff]], seguito dal suo Paggio che porta la sua [[Sword|spada]] e il brocchiere |
| SIR JOHN | Ehi tu, gigante, che dice il dottore delle mie [[Water|acque]]? |
| PAGGIO | Ha detto, signore, che l'[[Water|acqua]] in sé era una buona e sana [[Water|acqua]], ma che, quanto al soggetto cui apparteneva, poteva avere più malattie di quante ne sapesse. |
| SIR JOHN | Uomini d'ogni sorta si fanno un [[Pride|vanto]] di beffarmi. Il cervello di questa sciocca impastatura d'argilla, l'uomo, non è capace d'inventare cosa che muova al riso più di quanto io invento, o di quanto s'inventa su di me. Non solo sono arguto io stesso, ma sono causa che l'arguzia sia negli altri uomini. Cammino qui davanti a te come una scrofa che ha soffocato tutta la figliata tranne uno. Se il Principe t'ha messo al mio servizio per altra ragione che per farmi risaltare, ebbene, non ho giudizio. Tu, figlio di puttana d'una mandragola, sei più adatto a essere portato sul mio cappello che ad attendermi alle calcagna. Non fui mai servito da un'agata fino ad ora; ma non ti monterò né in oro né in argento, bensì in vile panno, e ti rimanderò al tuo padrone per un [[Jewel|gioiello]]—quel giovinetto del Principe tuo padrone, il cui mento non è ancora piumato. Mi crescerà prima la barba sul palmo della mano che a lui una sulla guancia; eppure non si perita di dire che la sua faccia è una faccia regale. Dio la finisca quando vorrà; non c'è ancora un pelo fuori posto. Può ben tenerla per faccia regale, ché un barbiere non ne caverà mai sei soldi. E tuttavia farà il gallo come se fosse uomo fatto fin da quando suo padre era scapolo. Può ben tenersi la sua grazia, ma della mia è quasi fuori, glielo posso assicurare. Che disse messer Dumbleton a proposito del raso per il mio mantello corto e le brache? |
| PAGGIO | Ha detto, signore, che dovreste procurargli miglior garanzia di Bardolph. Non ha voluto accettare né il suo né il vostro impegno; non gli piaceva la sicurtà. |
| SIR JOHN | Sia dannato come il ricco epulone! Voglia Dio che la sua lingua arda di più! Un figlio di puttana d'un Achitofel, un furfante da ‘sissignore-in-fede’, tenere sulla corda un gentiluomo e poi star lì a cavillare sulla sicurtà! Quei figli di puttana dalle teste lisce ora non portano altro che scarpe alte e mazzi di chiavi alla cintura; e se un uomo tratta con loro in onesta compera a credito, allora vengono fuori con la sicurtà. Preferirei che mi cacciassero in bocca il veleno per i topi piuttosto che tapparmela con la sicurtà. Mi aspettavo che m'avrebbe mandato ventidue iarde di raso, com'è vero che sono cavaliere, e mi manda ‘sicurtà’! [[Well|Bene]], può [[Sleep|dormire]] sicuro, ché ha il corno dell'abbondanza, e la leggerezza di sua moglie vi traluce attraverso; e tuttavia non ci vede, benché abbia la sua propria lanterna a fargli lume. Dov'è Bardolph? |
| PAGGIO | È andato a Smithfield a comprare un [[Horse|cavallo]] a vossignoria. |
| SIR JOHN | Io l'ho comprato a San Paolo, e lui mi comprerà un [[Horse|cavallo]] a Smithfield. Se solo potessi procurarmi una moglie al bordello, avrei servo, cavallo e moglie. |
| *(didascalia)* | Entrano il [[Lord Chief Justice|Lord Giudice Supremo]] e il suo Servo |
| PAGGIO | Signore, ecco che viene il nobiluomo che fece imprigionare il<br>Principe per averlo percosso a proposito di Bardolph. |
| *(didascalia)* | SIR JOHN allontanandosi Sta' vicino; non voglio vederlo. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | (al suo Servo) Chi è colui che passa di là? |
| SERVO | [[Falstaff]], se piace a vossignoria. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | Quello che era sotto inchiesta per la rapina? |
| SERVO | Lui, mio signore; ma da allora ha reso buon servigio a Shrewsbury, e, a quanto odo, ora va con qualche incarico presso Lord John di Lancaster. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | Come, a York? Richiamatelo indietro. |
| SERVO | Sir John [[Falstaff]]! |
| SIR JOHN | Ragazzo, digli che sono sordo. |
| PAGGIO | (al Servo) Dovete parlare più forte; il mio padrone è sordo. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | Sono certo che lo è per udire qualcosa di buono. (Al Servo) Va' a tirarlo per il gomito; devo parlargli. |
| SERVO | Sir John! |
| SIR JOHN | Come, un giovane furfante e accattone! Non ci sono forse guerre? Non c'è forse impiego? Non manca forse di sudditi il [[King|Re]]? Non hanno forse bisogno di soldati i ribelli? Benché sia una vergogna stare da altra parte che da una sola, è vergogna peggiore mendicare che stare dalla parte peggiore, fosse pur peggiore di quanto il nome di ribellione possa dire. |
| SERVO | Mi fraintendete, signore. |
| SIR JOHN | Ma come, signore, ho forse detto che eravate un uomo onesto? Messi da parte il mio grado di cavaliere e la mia qualità di soldato, avrei mentito per la gola se l'avessi detto. |
| SERVO | Vi prego dunque, signore, di mettere da parte il vostro grado di cavaliere e la vostra qualità di soldato, e datemi licenza di dirvi che mentite per la gola se dite che io sia altro che un uomo onesto. 88 |
| SIR JOHN | Ti do licenza di dirmelo? Metto da parte ciò che è cresciuto con me? Se ottieni licenza alcuna da me, impiccami. Se te la prendi da te, faresti meglio a essere impiccato. Segui la pista a rovescio. Via, sparisci! |
| SERVO | Signore, il mio signore vorrebbe parlarvi. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | Sir John [[Falstaff]], una parola con voi. |
| SIR JOHN | Mio buon signore! Dio conceda a vossignoria un buon [[Time|tempo]] di giornata. Sono lieto di vedere vossignoria fuori. Avevo sentito dire che vossignoria era malata. [[Hope|Spero]] che vossignoria esca per consiglio dei medici. Vossignoria, benché non sia del tutto uscita di gioventù, ha pure in sé qualche sentore d'età, qualche sapore del salso del [[Time|tempo]]; e umilissimamente supplico vossignoria d'avere riverente cura della propria salute. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | Sir John, vi mandai a chiamare prima della vostra spedizione a Shrewsbury. |
| SIR JOHN | Se piace a vossignoria, odo che sua maestà è tornata dal Galles con qualche malessere. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | Non parlo di sua maestà. Non voleste venire quando vi mandai a chiamare. |
| SIR JOHN | E odo, per di più, che sua altezza è caduta in questa stessa maledetta apoplessia. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | [[Well|Bene]], Dio lo guarisca! Vi prego, lasciate che vi parli. |
| SIR JOHN | Quest'apoplessia è, a quel ch'io intendo, una specie di letargia, se piace a vossignoria, una specie di sonnolenza nel [[Blood|sangue]], un maledetto formicolio. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | Che me ne dite a fare? Sia com'è. |
| SIR JOHN | Ha la sua origine da molto dolore, dallo studio, e dalla perturbazione del cervello. Ho letto in Galeno la causa dei suoi effetti. È una specie di sordità. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | Credo che voi siate caduto in quel male, ché non udite ciò che vi dico. |
| SIR JOHN | Molto [[Well|bene]], mio signore, molto [[Well|bene]]. Piuttosto, se vi piace, è la malattia del non ascoltare, il morbo del non far caso, quella di cui soffro. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | Punirvi per i calcagni emenderebbe l'attenzione dei vostri orecchi, e non m'importa di diventare il vostro medico. |
| SIR JOHN | Sono povero come Giobbe, mio signore, ma non altrettanto paziente.<br>Vossignoria può somministrarmi la pozione della [[Imprisonment|prigionia]] in ragione della [[Poverty|povertà]]; ma quanto al mio essere vostro paziente e seguire le vostre prescrizioni, i saggi potranno farne una dramma di scrupolo, o anzi uno scrupolo intero. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | Vi mandai a chiamare, quando c'erano accuse capitali contro di voi, perché veniste a parlarmi. |
| SIR JOHN | Poiché allora fui consigliato dal mio dotto avvocato nelle leggi di questo servizio di terra, non venni. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | [[Well|Bene]], la [[Truth|verità]] è, Sir John, che vivete in grande infamia. |
| SIR JOHN | Chi si affibbia la mia cintura non può vivere in meno. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | I vostri mezzi sono molto esili, e il vostro sperpero è grande. |
| SIR JOHN | Vorrei fosse altrimenti; vorrei che i miei mezzi fossero maggiori e la mia vita più esile. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | Avete traviato il giovane Principe. |
| SIR JOHN | È il giovane Principe che ha traviato me. Io sono il tale dal gran ventre, ed egli il mio cane. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | [[Well|Bene]], mi ripugna irritare una ferita appena rimarginata. Il vostro servigio diurno a Shrewsbury ha un poco indorato la vostra impresa [[Night|notturna]] a Gads [[Hill]]. Potete ringraziare l'inquieto [[Time|tempo]] se siete passato quieto oltre quell'azione. |
| SIR JOHN | Mio signore— |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | Ma poiché tutto va [[Well|bene]], così mantenetelo. Non svegliate un lupo che dorme. |
| SIR JOHN | Svegliare un lupo è brutto quanto fiutare una volpe. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | Come! Voi siete come una candela, la parte migliore già consumata. |
| SIR JOHN | Una candela da baldoria, mio signore, tutta sego—se dicessi di cera, la mia mole ne proverebbe la [[Truth|verità]]. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | Non c'è un pelo bianco sul vostro viso che non dovrebbe avere il suo effetto di gravità. |
| SIR JOHN | Il suo effetto di sugo, sugo, sugo. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | Voi seguite su e giù il giovane Principe come il suo angelo cattivo. |
| SIR JOHN | Non è così, mio signore; il vostro angelo cattivo è leggero, ma [[Hope|spero]] che chi mi guarda mi prenda senza pesarmi. Eppure sotto qualche riguardo, lo concedo, non ho corso. Non so che dire, la virtù è tenuta in così poco conto in questi tempi da ortolani che il vero valore è diventato guardiano d'orsi; l'ingegno pronto è fatto oste, e la sua vivacità sciupata a far conti; tutti gli altri doni che appartengono all'uomo, come la malizia di quest'età li foggia, non valgono un'uva spina. Voi che siete vecchi non considerate le capacità di noi che siamo giovani. Voi misurate il calore dei nostri fegati con l'amarezza dei vostri fieli. E noi che siamo all'avanguardia della nostra giovinezza, devo confessarlo, siamo anche scapestrati. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | Iscrivete voi il vostro nome nel ruolo della gioventù, voi che siete registrato vecchio con tutti i caratteri dell'età? Non avete forse l'occhio umido, la mano secca, la guancia gialla, la barba bianca, la gamba che scema, il ventre che cresce? Non è forse rotta la vostra voce, corto il vostro [[Wind|fiato]], [[Double|doppio]] il vostro mento, semplice il vostro ingegno, e ogni parte di voi devastata dall'antichità? E ancora vorrete chiamarvi giovane? Vergogna, vergogna, vergogna, Sir John! |
| *(didascalia)* | SIR JOHN Mio signore, nacqui verso le tre dell'[[Clock|orologio]] del pomeriggio con la testa bianca, e alquanto un ventre rotondo. Quanto alla voce, l'ho persa a gridare e a cantare antifone. Per provare oltre la mia giovinezza, non lo farò. La [[Truth|verità]] è che sono vecchio soltanto di giudizio e d'intelletto; e chi vuol far salti con me per mille marchi, mi presti il denaro, e me la vedrò con lui! Quanto allo schiaffo che il Principe vi diede, ve lo diede da principe villano, e voi lo prendeste da signore assennato. L'ho rimproverato per questo, e il giovane [[Lion|leone]] se ne pente—a parte in verità, non in cenere e cilicio, ma in seta nuova e vin di Spagna vecchio. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | [[Well|Bene]], Dio mandi al Principe un miglior compagno! |
| SIR JOHN | Dio mandi al compagno un miglior principe! Non riesco a levarmelo dalle mani. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | [[Well|Bene]], il [[King|Re]] ha separato voi e il principe Harry. Odo che andate con Lord John di Lancaster contro l'Arcivescovo e il [[Earl of Northumberland|Conte di Northumberland]]. |
| SIR JOHN | Sì, e ne ringrazio il vostro grazioso dolce ingegno. Ma badate di pregare, voi tutti che baciate la mia signora Pace a [[Home|casa]], che i nostri eserciti non si scontrino in un giorno caldo; ché, per il Signore, io porto con me soltanto due camicie, e non intendo sudare straordinariamente. Se sarà un giorno caldo e brandirò altro che la mia bottiglia, ch'io non possa mai più sputare bianco. Non c'è azione pericolosa che possa far capolino senza che io ci sia cacciato dentro. [[Well|Bene]], non posso durare per sempre. Ma è sempre stato il vezzo della nostra nazione inglese, se ha una cosa buona, di renderla troppo comune. Se proprio volete dire che sono un vecchio, dovreste darmi riposo. Volesse Dio che il mio nome non fosse così terribile al nemico com'è. Meglio essere divorato a [[Death|morte]] dalla ruggine che essere consumato a nulla dal moto perpetuo. |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | [[Well|Bene]], siate onesto, siate onesto, e Dio benedica la vostra spedizione. |
| SIR JOHN | Vuol vossignoria prestarmi mille sterline per equipaggiarmi? |
| [[Lord Chief Justice|LORD GIUDICE SUPREMO]] | Non un penny, non un penny. Siete troppo impaziente per portar croci. State [[Well|bene]]. Salutatemi mio cugino Westmorland. |
| *(didascalia)* | Escono il [[Lord Chief Justice|Lord Giudice Supremo]] e il suo Servo |
| SIR JOHN | Se lo faccio, che mi si dia un buffetto con un maglio da tre uomini. Un uomo non può separare la vecchiaia dall'avarizia più di quanto possa dividere le giovani membra dalla lussuria; ma la gotta tormenta l'una e il mal francese pizzica l'altra, e così entrambi i gradi prevengono le mie maledizioni. Ragazzo! |
| PAGGIO | Signore. |
| SIR JOHN | Che denaro c'è nella mia borsa? |
| PAGGIO | Sette grossi e due penny. |
| *(didascalia)* | SIR JOHN Non trovo rimedio contro questa consunzione della borsa. Il prendere a prestito la tira in lungo e la trascina, ma il male è incurabile. (Porgendo lettere) Va' a portare questa lettera al mio signore di Lancaster; questa al Principe; questa al Conte di Westmorland; e questa alla vecchia madama Ursula, che ho giurato ogni settimana di sposare da quando m'accorsi del primo pelo bianco sul mento. Sbrigati. Sai dove trovarmi. Esce il Paggio Peste di questa gotta!—o gotta di questa peste!—ché l'una o l'altra fa il birbante col mio alluce. Non importa se zoppico; ho le guerre per pretesto, e la mia pensione parrà tanto più ragionevole. Un buon ingegno saprà servirsi di tutto. Volgerò le malattie in profitto. Esce |
